Projective contact log varieties
Adrian Langer

Abstract
We study contact structures on smooth complex projective varieties with a simple normal crossing divisor, generalizing some well-known results concerning the non-logarithmic case. In particular, we describe the structure of elementary log contractions of such log varieties and we construct the corresponding contact structures.



Address:

Institute of Mathematics, University of Warsaw,
ul. Banacha 2, 02-097 Warszawa, Poland

e-mail:
alan@mimuw.edu.pl

Keywords:
complex projective variety; contact structure; Lie algebroid; log contraction; simple normal crossing divisor

Introduction

Let
X
be a smooth complex projective variety. A
contact structure
on
X
is defined as a corank
1
subbundle
{\mathcal{F}}
of the tangent bundle
T_{X}
, where the pairing
{\mathcal{F}}\times{\mathcal{F}}\to T_{X}/{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{L}}
induced by the Lie bracket is everywhere non-degenerate. In this situation,
X
must have odd dimension
2r+1
. Dually, one can consider an
{\mathcal{L}}
-valued
1
-form
\theta\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}\otimes{\mathcal{L}})
. This form defines a contact structure if and only if the expression
\theta\wedge(d\theta)^{\wedge r}
, which is a well-defined section of
\Omega_{X}^{2r+1}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}^{r}
, has no zeroes.

Contact structures first emerged in real geometry and later became a significant area of study in complex geometry. In the complex setting, one of the motivations came from the work
[
LS94
]
of C. LeBrun and S. Salamon, who proved that twistor spaces of compact quaternion-Kähler manifolds with positive scalar curvature are contact Fano varieties (see
[
LS94
]
). They also conjectured that every such variety is homogeneous. Although there has been some recent progress on this conjecture (see
[
BW22
]
and
[
ORCW21
]
), it still remains open.

One of the central classification results for contact complex varieties comes from the combined results of
[
KPSW00
]
and
[
Dem02
]
. These works show that a projective contact variety is either the projectivization of the tangent bundle of a smooth projective variety or it is Fano with
b_{2}=1
.

The main aim of this paper is to study generalization of this result to the logarithmic case. We assume that all varieties are defined over a fixed algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
0
and we consider contact structures on a projective snc pair, i.e., a pair consisting of a smooth projective variety
X

and a simple normal crossing divisor
D
. In this case a contact structure is a corank
1
subbundle
{\mathcal{F}}
of the logarithmic tangent bundle
T_{X}(-\log D)
such that the pairing
{\mathcal{F}}\times{\mathcal{F}}\to T_{X}(-\log D)/{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{L}}
induced by the Lie bracket is everywhere non-degenerate. We usually forget about
{\mathcal{F}}
and we think about the contact structure as the projection
T_{X}(-\log D)\to{\mathcal{L}}
. Dually, one can consider an
{\mathcal{L}}
-valued logarithmic
1
-form
\theta\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D)\otimes{\mathcal{L}})
. This form is contact if and only if
\theta\wedge(d\theta)^{\wedge r}
has no zeroes.
(X,D)
is called
contact
if it admits a contact structure (or equivalently a contact form). Such structure is a natural compactification of a contact structure on the quasi-projective variety
U=X\backslash{\mathop{{\rm Supp\,}}}D
.

It is natural to expect that, similar to the usual case, projective contact snc pairs can also be classified.
It is straightforward to show that the projectivization of the logarithmic tangent bundle of a projective snc pair is contact (see Proposition
6.1
). In this case, the contact form is given by a logarithmic version of Liouville’s
1
-form (cf.
[
Arn89
, Appendix 4 D]
for the classical case).

One of the key surprises in this theory is the existence of contact projective snc pairs with
b_{2}\geq 2
, where the contact structure is not related to such standard contact structures. This phenomenon can be explained as follows. It is well-known in differential geometry and partial differential equations that higher jet bundles of a given vector bundle on a manifold carry a canonical contact form associated with the Cartan distribution (see, e.g.,
[
Sau89
]
).
This contact structure differs in that it is given by a distribution
{\mathcal{F}}\subset T_{X}
, where
T_{X}/{\mathcal{F}}
is a vector bundle of possibly higher rank than
1
. However, in the case of the first jet bundle of a line bundle it gives the usual contact structure (see, e.g.,
[
MS98
, Example 3.44]
for the case of the trivial line bundle). The corresponding contact form is not homogeneous with respect to fiber coordinates, but it extends to a contact form on the natural log variety underlying certain projective bundle compactifying this jet bundle. This explains how log contact varieties of dimension
(2r+1)
can arise as projective bundles over
r
-dimensional varieties. More generally, for any projective snc pair
(Y,B)
of dimension
d\leq r
and any collection of
(r+1-d)
line bundles on
Y
, we provide an explicit construction of a contact snc pair of dimension
(2r+1)
, describing it as a projective bundle over
Y
(see Proposition
6.3
).
In particular, the classification of projective contact snc pairs is significantly more complex than that of usual contact varieties.

What is surprising from this point of view is that our construction describes essentially all new contact structures. More precisely, we prove the following theorem, which is a special case of Theorem
5.14
.
The
c_{1}^{\log}({\mathcal{M}})
in this theorem denotes the image of the first Chern class
c_{1}({\mathcal{M}})\in H^{1}(Y,\Omega_{Y}^{1})
in

H^{1}(Y,\Omega_{Y}^{1}(\log B))={\mathop{{\rm Ext\,}}}^{1}(T_{Y}(-\log B),{\mathcal{O}}_{Y})
.

Theorem 0.1
.

Let
(X,D)
be a contact projective snc pair of dimension
2r+1
. Then
-(K_{X}+D)
is not nef and
X
admits an elementary log contraction
\varphi:X\to Y
for which
-(K_{X}+D)
is
\varphi
-ample. If the restriction of
\varphi
to
X\backslash{\mathop{{\rm Supp\,}}}D
is not an isomorphism then either
(X,D)
is log Fano with
b_{2}(X)=1
or

Y
is smooth and there exists a simple normal crossing divisor
B
on
Y

such that one of the following occurs:

1.

\dim Y=r+1
and
(X\to Y)\simeq({\mathbb{P}}(T_{Y}(-\log B))\to Y
) and
D=\varphi^{-1}(B)
,

2.

\dim Y=r+1-s
for some
1\leq s\leq r
and there exists
s
line bundles
{\mathcal{M}}_{1},...,{\mathcal{M}}_{s}
on
{Y}

such that
(X\to Y)\simeq({\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{E}})\to Y)
, where we have an extension

0\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}{\mathcal{M}}_{i}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{E}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{C}}\to 0,

in which
{\mathcal{C}}
is the extension of
T_{Y}(-\log B)
by
{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}^{\oplus s}
given by

-\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}c_{1}^{\log}({\mathcal{M}}_{i})
. In this case
D
is the sum of
\varphi^{-1}(B)

and
s
divisors determined by the cokernels of
{\mathcal{M}}_{i}\to{\mathcal{E}}
.

Our main contribution is description of log contractions and construction of contact structures in the corresponding cases. The first part of the above theorem is a simple corollary of the result of F. Touzet
[
Tou16
]
(see Corollary
4.12
) generalizing earlier result of J.-P. Demailly
[
Dem02
]
to the log case. In fact, this result shows that
K_{X}+D
is not pseudoeffective. So by
[
BDPP13
, Theorem 2]
there exist on
X
strongly moving curves
C
with
(K_{X}+D)\cdot C<0
. In this case the log Bend and Break conjecture
[
KM99
, Conjecture 1.11]
of S. Keel and J. McKernan predicts that the quasi-projective variety
U=X\backslash{\mathop{{\rm Supp\,}}}D
is
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
-uniruled (in the sense of, e.g.,
[
CZ19
, Definition 1.2]
). In case
(X,D)
admits an elementary log contraction which is not an isomorphism on the open part
U=X\backslash{\mathop{{\rm Supp\,}}}D
, this fact follows easily from the above result.

We also prove a far reaching generalizations of Theorem
0.1
that describe a precise structure of all good log contractions of contact snc pairs. Unlike in the case of contact varieties, there also appear birational log contractions. In this case the whole exceptional locus is contained in the support of
D
and the target is often singular. But divisorial contractions can also lead to snc pairs, e.g., we can obtain a contact snc pair by blowing up the fiber of
{\mathbb{P}}(T_{Y}(-\log B))\to Y
over a point that does not belong to the support of
B
(see Example
7.6
).

To study log contractions of contact snc pairs, it is essential to explore more general snc pairs with what is called a
semi-contact structure
(see Definition
4.5
). A semi-contact structure is defined as a contact structure on some special Lie algebroid generalizing a Picard Lie algebroid in the sense of A. Beilinson and J. Bernstein
[
BB93
, Definition 2.1.3]
. This structure enables the description of the restriction of the contact structure on the pair
(X,D)
to the strata of the stratification of
X
induced by the components of
D
. A clear advantage of this approach is that it allows for inductive reasoning on the dimension, whereas conventional contact structures only exist in odd dimensions.

The classification of contact varieties with
b_{2}\geq 2
in
[
KPSW00
]
used Mori’s theory, specifically focusing on contractions and rational curves on contact varieties. Therefore, it is natural to approach the classification of contact snc pairs using log contractions and the non-proper analogue of rational curves suggested by S. Keel and J. McKernan in
[
KM99
]
(see also
[
CZ19
]
). However, many standard results do not directly translate to the log case, and to prove Theorem
0.1
, we must formulate appropriate analogues that are applicable in the log setting. Specifically, we require a relative version of Keel–McKernan’s log bend-and-break
[
KM99
, Corollary 5.4]
, a log version of the Ionescu–Wiśniewski inequality, and a log version of the rank computation for
{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X)
(see
[
Kol96
, Chapter II, Proposition 3.10]
for the standard case). Additionally, several results concerning Lie algebroids and generalized Atiyah extensions are extensively used in this paper.
With these tools, we attempt to mirror the proof from
[
KPSW00
]
, though the process becomes quite intricate, leading to various additional challenges that are typically resolved through a careful study of specific loci and the use of dimensional induction.

A contact structure can more generally be defined on a log smooth log variety in the sense of Fontaine–Illusie–Kato (see
[
Kat89
]
and
[
Ogu18
]
). In this context, the contact structure is defined by the same data as in teh usual case, with the only difference being the replacement of a smooth variety with
a log smooth log variety. These structures also appear as natural compactifications of contact structures on quasi-projective varieties. In this paper, we focus on contact snc pairs, but analogous results can also be proven in the logarithmic case. Unfortunately, this requires significant additional work, which would obscure the main ideas, so we have chosen to first explain the classical case and address the logarithmic case in a separate paper.

As mentioned earlier, log contractions of contact snc pairs (or log smooth log varieties) that are not of fiber type often result in singular varieties. The study of singular contact log varieties and contact log Fano log varieties with
b_{2}=1
is also deferred to another paper. We also leave out potential applications of the above theory in complex or real geometry.

In this paper we focus on algebraic varieties. This approach does not restrict the generality of the obtained results, as complex projective manifolds are well-known to be algebraic. It should also be noted that much of this paper could be written in the language of complex manifolds, but we prefer to use the language of algebraic geometry. While this choice might seem unnecessary to some readers (and it can make the paper more difficult to read), there are mathematical reasons for doing so. One reason is that in some cases, our study leads to singular (projective) varieties, and singularities in complex geometry are not as rigid as those of algebraic varieties. However, the most important reason is that we aim to show that contact projective (log) varieties have a well-defined local algebraic structure. This might be surprising, as it is well-known that Darboux’s theorem, which describes the local structure of a symplectic or contact manifold, does not apply in algebraic local coordinates.

Relation to other work

Contact structures are well-known to be related to symplectic structures. Rather recently, differential geometers started to study a generalization of symplectic structures under the name of b-symplectic manifolds. These structures first appeared in
[
GMP14
]
and they are described in the language of b-geometry refering to Melrose’s calculus for differential operators on real manifolds with (smooth) boundary (see
[
Mel93
]
). Complex analogues of symplectic structures in case of complex manifolds with boundary have also been heavily studied under the name of log symplectic manifolds (see, e.g.,
[
CG18
]
,
[
Got02
]
,
[
Got16
]
and
[
Pym17
]
).

Even more recently, b-contact manifolds were defined in
[
MO23
]
(see also
[
BDM+19
]
). The definition is the same as in the case of usual manifolds but it replaces manifolds with b-manifolds (tangent bundle with b-tangent bundle etc.). Our contact snc pairs are an obvious analogue of such structures in complex geometry but it seems they have not been studied till now. Let us also note that our constructions work in the real case giving a new construction of (compact) b-contact manifolds that compactify
1
-jet bundles (see Appendix B). It seems that this construction has not been noticed before.

The structure of the paper is as follows. In Section 1 we generalize Wahl’s theorem on ample line bundles of a tangent bundle to the logarithmic case and we recall some results on the logarithmic cone and contraction theorems. In Section 2 we adapt several theorems on rational curves on smooth projective varieties to the log case. In Section 3, we extend contact and symplectic structures to the Lie algebroid case and prove a few auxiliary results. Then in Section 4 we introduce special Lie algebroids that generalize Picard algebroids and we examine their contact structures. Section 5 is dedicated to proving the main result. In Section 6 we present new constructions of contact snc pairs, while in Section 7 we investigate contact projective snc pairs with two elementary log contractions, demonstrating the abundance of such varieties. The appendices provide a classification of semi-contact snc pairs in small dimensions (Appendix A) and demonstrate how analogues of the Section 6 constructions yield new b-contact b-manifolds (Appendix B).

Notation

In the whole paper, we assume that our varieties are defined over an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
0
. A variety is always irreducible. Since all structures in this paper are
k
-linear, by abuse of notation we write
\Omega_{X}^{1}
for the sheaf of Kähler differentials
\Omega_{X/k}
and
T_{X}
for the sheaf
T_{X/k}=Der_{k}({\mathcal{O}}_{X},{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
of
k
-linear derivations of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
. For a coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module
{\mathcal{E}}
we write
{\mathcal{E}}^{*}
for
{\mathop{{\mathcal{H}}om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
. All tensor operations on
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules are performed over
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
unless otherwise stated explicitly.

A
vector bundle
on
X
is a locally free coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module of finite rank.

A
contraction
is a proper surjective map
\varphi:X\to Y
of normal varieties such that
\varphi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}={\mathcal{O}}_{Y}
(or equivalently
\varphi
has connected fibers).

1
Preliminaries

In this section we generalize Wahl’s characterization of projective spaces
[
Wah83
]
to log varieties, recall the logarithmic cone (and contraction) theorem and prove some auxiliary results on log extremal curves.

1.1
Logarithmic version of Wahl’s theorem

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
(X,D)
be a projective snc pair and let
{\mathcal{L}}
be an ample line bundle on
X
. Let
n=\dim X\geq 1
.
If
H^{0}(X,T_{X}(-\log D)\otimes{\mathcal{L}}^{-1})\neq 0
then either
(X,{\mathcal{L}})\simeq({\mathbb{P}}^{1},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(2))
and
D=0
or

(X,{\mathcal{L}})\simeq({\mathbb{P}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(1))
and
D
is an arrangement of
s\leq n
hyperplanes in a general position
(i.e., it forms an snc divisor).

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
n
with
n=1
being clear.
Since
T_{X}(-\log D)\subset T_{X}
, the first part follows immediately from Wahl’s theorem
[
Wah83
, Theorem 1]
. So we can assume that
(X,{\mathcal{L}})=({\mathbb{P}}^{n},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(1))
and
D\neq 0
.
For any irreducible component
D_{i}
of
D
we have a short exact sequence

0\to T_{D_{i}}(-\log(D-D_{i})|_{D_{i}})\to T_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(-\log D)|_{D_{i}}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{D_{i}}(-(D-D_{i}))\to 0.

By assumption we have a nonzero map
{\mathcal{L}}\to T_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(-\log D)
. Its restriction to
D_{i}
gives a nonzero
map
{\mathcal{L}}|_{D_{i}}\to T_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(-\log D)|_{D_{i}}
. Since there are no nonzero maps from
{\mathcal{L}}|_{D_{i}}
to

{\mathcal{O}}_{D_{i}}(-(D-D_{i}))
, we have an induced non-zero map
{\mathcal{L}}|_{D_{i}}\to T_{D_{i}}(-\log(D-D_{i})|_{D_{i}})
. Using the induction assumption we see that
D_{i}
is a hyperplane and
(D-D_{i})|_{D_{i}}
consists of at most
(n-1)
hyperplanes in
D_{i}\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
. This easily implies the required assertion.
∎

Remark 1.2
.

Let
D
be an arrangement of
1\leq s\leq n
hyperplanes in a general position in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
. Then
by
[
DK93
, Proposition 2.10]
we have

T_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(-\log D)\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}^{\oplus(s-1)}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(1)^{\oplus(n-s+1)}.

This shows that the above theorem is optimal. Note also that
D
is projectively equivalent
to the standard hyperplane arrangement given by
x_{1}...x_{s}=0
. In particular, intersection of any subset of hyperplanes in
D
is connected.

The above theorem and the main result of M. Andreatta and J. Wiśniewski
[
AW01
, Theorem]
immediately imply the following corollary:

Corollary 1.3
.

Let
(X,D)
be a projective snc pair with
n=\dim X\geq 1
and let
{\mathcal{E}}
be an ample vector bundle contained in
T_{X}(-\log D)
. Then we have the following possibilities for
(X,D)
and
{\mathcal{E}}
:

1.

(X,D)\simeq({\mathbb{P}}^{n},0)
and
{\mathcal{E}}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(1)^{\oplus m}
for some
m\leq n
,

2.

(X,D)\simeq({\mathbb{P}}^{n},0)
and
{\mathcal{E}}\simeq T_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}
,

3.

X\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
,
D
is an arrangement of
1\leq s\leq n
hyperplanes and
{\mathcal{E}}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(1)^{\oplus m}
for some
m\leq{(n-s+1)}
.

1.2
Logarithmic cone theorem

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety.
Let
Z_{1}(X)_{{\mathbb{R}}}
be the real vector space generated by integral curves
on
X
, and let
N_{1}(X)
be the space
Z_{1}(X)_{{\mathbb{R}}}
modulo numerical equivalence. We denote by

N^{1}(X)
the space of real Cartier divisors modulo numerical equivalence. We also set

\rho(X):=\dim_{{\mathbb{R}}}N^{1}(X).

Let
NE(X)\subset N_{1}(X)
be the convex cone generated by classes
of effective
1
-cycles and let
\overline{NE}(X)
be its closure. For

L\in N^{1}(X)
, we use the notation

\overline{NE}(X)_{L\geq 0}:=\bigl\{z\in\overline{NE}(X)\mid L\cdot z\geq 0\bigr\}

and

N_{1}(X)_{L<0}:=\bigl\{z\in N_{1}(X)\mid L\cdot z<0\bigr\}.

A subcone
F\subset\overline{NE}(X)
is called an
extremal face

if, whenever
z_{1},z_{2}\in\overline{NE}(X)
and
z_{1}+z_{2}\in F
, one has

z_{1},z_{2}\in F
. It is called
(K_{X}+D)
-negative
if

(K_{X}+D)\cdot z<0

for every nonzero
z\in F
. A one-dimensional extremal face is called an

extremal ray
.
A
(K_{X}+D)
-negative extremal face
F
is called a
(K_{X}+D)
-extremal face
.

A nef Cartier divisor
H_{F}
is called a
supporting divisor
of an
extremal face
F
if

F=\bigl\{z\in\overline{NE}(X)\mid H_{F}\cdot z=0\bigr\}.

We need the following version of the cone theorem that follows from the cone theorem for dlt pairs (see
[
KM98
, Theorems 3.25(2) and 3.35]
and
[
Kol96
, Chapter III, Corollary 1.4]
).

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
(X,D)
be a projective snc pair. Then:

1.

There are countably many rational curves
C_{j}\subset X
such that
(K_{X}+D)\cdot C_{j}<0
and

\overline{NE}(X)=\overline{NE}(X)_{K_{X}+D\geq 0}+\sum_{j}{\mathbb{R}}^{+}[C_{j}].

The rays
{\mathbb{R}}^{+}[C_{j}]\subset N_{1}(X)
are locally discrete in
N_{1}(X)_{K_{X}+D<0}
and every extremal ray
R
contained in
N_{1}(X)_{K_{X}+D<0}
is of the form
R={\mathbb{R}}^{+}[C_{j}]
for some
j
.

2.

For any
(K_{X}+D)
-extremal face
F

there is a unique contraction
\varphi_{F}:X\to Y
to projective
Y
such that an irreducible curve
C\subset X
is mapped by
\varphi_{F}
to a point if and only if
[C]\in F
.

3.

Every
(K_{X}+D)
-negative extremal face
F
admits a supporting
divisor
H_{F}
. Moreover,

mH_{F}-(K_{X}+D)

is ample for all sufficiently large
m
.

A contraction
\varphi:X\to Y
is called a
good log contraction
of
(X,D)
if
-(K_{X}+D)
is
\varphi
-ample. Any good log contraction is of the form
\varphi_{F}
for some extremal face
F
(see
[
KMM87
, Lemma 3.2.5]
).
A
1
-dimensional
(K_{X}+D)
-extremal face is called a

(K_{X}+D)
-extremal ray
. If
R
is a
(K_{X}+D)
-extremal ray then the good log contraction
\varphi_{R}
is called
elementary
.

Let
(X,D)
be a projective snc pair such that
(K_{X}+D)
is not nef. Then either
\rho(X)=1
and
-(K_{X}+D)
is ample (the log Fano case) or
\rho(X)\geq 2
and there exists a good elementary log contraction
\varphi:X\to Y
with
\dim Y>0
.

If
D=\sum_{i\in I}D_{i}
for some smooth irreducible
D_{i}
then we write
D_{J}:=\bigcap_{j\in J}D_{j}
for
J\subset I
. In particular, we set
D_{\emptyset}=X
. For a subset
J\subset I
we denote by
D^{J}
the restriction of the divisor

\sum_{i\in I\backslash J}D_{i}
to
D_{J}
.
Let us note the following complement to the cone theorem:

Proposition 1.5
.

Let
D=\sum_{i\in I}D_{i}
and let us fix some
J\subset I
(possibly
\emptyset
).
If
F
is a
(K_{X}+D)
-extremal face and
C\subset D_{J}
is a curve such that
[C]\in F

then we can find a rational curve
C^{\prime}\subset D_{J}
such that
[C^{\prime}]\in F
and

0<-(K_{X}+D)\cdot C^{\prime}\leq\dim D_{J}+1.

Moreover, if
C\not\subset D_{i}
for
i\in I\backslash J
then we can assume that
C\cap C^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
and

C^{\prime}\not\subset D_{i}
for
i\in I\backslash J
.

Proof.

Enlarging
J
if necessary we can assume that
J
is the set of all
j\in I
for which
C\subset D_{j}
. Then we have

0>(K_{X}+D)\cdot C=(K_{D_{J}}+D^{J})\cdot C\geq K_{D_{J}}\cdot C.

Let
H_{F}
be a supporting divisor for
F
. By
[
Kol96
, Chapter II, Theorem 5.8]
for any closed point
x\in C
we can find a rational curve
C^{\prime}
passing through
x
and such that

H_{F}\cdot C^{\prime}\leq 2\dim D_{J}\frac{H_{F}\cdot C}{-K_{D_{J}}\cdot C}=0.

Then
H_{F}\cdot C^{\prime}=0
and hence
[C^{\prime}]\in F
. This shows that we can assume that
C
is rational.
By
[
Kol96
, Chapter II, Theorem 5.7]
for any
x\in C\subset D_{J}
there is an effective
1
-cycle
\sum a_{i}C_{i}
in
D_{J}
whose irreducible components are rational curves such that
[C]
is effectively algebraically equivalent to
\sum a_{i}C_{i}
,
-K_{D_{J}}\cdot C_{i}\leq\dim D_{J}+1
for all
i
, and
x\in{\mathop{{\rm Supp\,}}}\sum a_{i}C_{i}
. Since
0=H_{F}\cdot C=\sum a_{i}H_{F}\cdot C_{i}\geq a_{i}H_{F}\cdot C_{i}\geq 0
, we see that

H_{F}\cdot C_{i}=0
and hence
[C_{i}]\in F
. Since
x\in{\mathop{{\rm Supp\,}}}\sum a_{i}C_{i}
we can also take
i
so that
x\in C_{i}
. Choosing
x
so that
x\not\in D_{j}
for
j\in I\backslash J
, we obtain

0<-(K_{X}+D)\cdot C_{i}=-(K_{D_{J}}+D^{J})\cdot C_{i}\leq-K_{D_{J}}\cdot C_{i}\leq\dim D_{J}+1.

∎

For a good log contraction
\varphi
we define its
length

l_{D}(\varphi)
as the minimum of
-(K_{X}+D)\cdot C
taken among all rational curves
C
contracted by
\varphi
. If the minimum is attained for
C
then we call
C
an
extremal rational curve
.
By Proposition
1.5
we have
1\leq l_{D}(\varphi)\leq\dim X+1.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety and let
{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X)
be the scheme parametrizing morphisms from
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
to
X
. This scheme comes with the evaluation morphism
F:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X)\to X
. For any family
V\subset{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X)
we write
F_{V}
for the restriction of
F
to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times V
. We denote by
{\rm locus}(V)
the image of
F_{V}
.
The family
V
is called
unsplit
if curves, obtained as images of morphisms from this family, cannot be deformed to a
1
-cycle consisting of more than one curve (counting multplicities). We refer to
[
Kol96
, Chapter IV, Definition 2.1]
for a precise definition.

Lemma 1.6
.

If
l_{D}(\varphi)=\dim X+1
then
X\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
and
D=0
.
In particular, if
D\neq 0
then
l_{D}(\varphi)\leq\dim X
.

Proof.

Assume that
l_{D}(\varphi)=\dim X+1
. Since any good log contraction factors through an elementary good log contraction, we can assume that
\varphi
is elementary.

Then for any extremal rational curve
C
contracted by
\varphi
we have
-(K_{X}+D)\cdot C=\dim X+1
. So
C\not\subset{\mathop{{\rm Supp\,}}}D
by Proposition
1.5
. In particular,
-K_{X}\cdot C\geq-(K_{X}+D)\cdot C=\dim X+1
. Let
f:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to C
be the normalization and let
V
be an irreducible component of
{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X)
containing
[f]
.
The family
V
is unsplit. Otherwise, a reducible degeneration would have components whose classes lie in the same extremal ray, and one component would have
-(K_{X}+D)
-degree strictly smaller than
l_{D}(\varphi)
.

By Ionescu–Wiśniewski’s inequality (see, e.g.,
[
Kol96
, Chapter IV, Corollary 2.6]
),
-K_{X}\cdot C=\dim X+1
and
\overline{{\rm locus}(V)}=X
. If
D=0
then
\varphi
contracts
X
to a point and hence
X
is Fano. Then
X\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
by
[
CMSB02
]
and
[
Keb02
, Theorem 1.1]
. If
D\neq 0
then

D\cdot C=0
and there exists
[g]\in V
such that the intersection of
C^{\prime}=g({\mathbb{P}}^{1})
with
D
is non-empty.
Since
D\cdot C^{\prime}=D\cdot C=0
,
C^{\prime}
is contained in
{\mathop{{\rm Supp\,}}}D
. But
{\mathbb{R}}^{+}[C^{\prime}]={\mathbb{R}}^{+}[C]
, so we get a contradiction with Proposition
1.5
. ∎

The proof of the following lemma follows standard arguments used, e.g., in the proof of
[
Kaw97
, Theorem 1.6]
.

Lemma 1.7
.

Let
(X,D)
be a projective snc pair and let
\varphi:X\to Y
be a good log contraction of
(X,D)
. Let
Z
be an irreducible component of
D
and let
D^{Z}
be the restriction of
(D-Z)
to
Z
. Then
T=\varphi(Z)
is normal and
\varphi|_{Z}:Z\to T
is a good log contraction of
(Z,D^{Z})
.

Proof.

Since
-(K_{X}+D)
is
\varphi
-ample, for small
\epsilon>0
the divisor

-(K_{X}+D)+\epsilon(D-Z)
is also
\varphi
-ample. Since

-Z=K_{X}+(-(K_{X}+D)+\epsilon(D-Z))+(1-\epsilon)(D-Z),

the relative version of the Kawamata-Viehweg vanishing theorem implies that

R^{i}\varphi_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(-Z))=0
for all
i>0
.
In particular, the map
{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}=\varphi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to\varphi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}
is surjective.
So if
T=\varphi(Z)
then
{\mathcal{O}}_{T}\to\varphi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}
is surjective. Since it is also injective, it is an isomorphism and hence
T
is normal. To see that
\varphi|_{Z}
is a good log contraction it is sufficient to note that

K_{Z}+D^{Z}=(K_{X}+D)|_{Z}
.
∎

By induction the above lemma implies the following corollary.

Corollary 1.8
.

Let
\varphi:X\to Y
be a good log contraction of a projective snc pair
(X,D)
with
D=\sum_{i\in I}D_{i}
. Let us fix
J\subset I
and an irreducible component
Z
of
D_{J}
. Then
T=\varphi(Z)
is normal and
\varphi|_{Z}:Z\to T
is a good log contraction of
(Z,D^{J}|_{Z})
.

2
Rational curves on snc pairs

In this section we generalize several results on rational curves on smooth projective varieties to projective snc pairs.

2.1
Log Bend-and-Break

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety. For any closed point
x\in X
we consider the subscheme

{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X;0\to x)

of
{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X)
parametrizing
g:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to X
mapping
0\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
to
x\in X
. For a closed point
x\in{\rm locus}(V)
, we write
V^{x}
for the family
V\cap{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X;0\to x)
and
{\rm locus}(V,x)
for the locus of
V^{x}
.

Now let
(X,D)
be a projective snc pair and let us set
U:=X\backslash{\mathop{{\rm Supp\,}}}D
. Let

\{D_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be the set of irreducible components of
D
and let
G=\{G_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a collection of effective Cartier divisors on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. Then we can consider
the closed subscheme
{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X;G\subset D)
of
{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X)
parametrizing morphisms
g:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to X
such that for every irreducible component
D_{i}
of
D
we have
G_{i}\subset g^{*}D_{i}
(see
[
KM99
, 5.1]
).
For any
x\in U
,
{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X;G\subset D,0\to x)
denotes the scheme-theoretic intersection of the schemes
{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X;G\subset D)
and
{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X;0\to x)
inside
{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X)
.

Let
f:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to X
be a fixed morphism such that
f({\mathbb{P}}^{1})\not\subset{\mathop{{\rm Supp\,}}}D
and
G_{i}\subset f^{*}D_{i}
for all
i\in I
. Let
W
be an irreducible component of
{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X;G\subset D)
containing
[f]
. Then

[
KM99
, Proposition 5.3]
implies that

\dim_{[f]}W\geq\chi({\mathbb{P}}^{1},f^{*}T_{X})-\deg G=-\deg f^{*}K_{X}-\deg G+\dim X.

Moreover, if
G_{i}=f^{*}D_{i}
for all
i\in I
then

T_{[f]}{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X;G\subset D)=H^{0}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},f^{*}T_{X}(-\log D)).

The proof of the following proposition follows that of
[
KM99
, Corollary 5.4]

and its improvement
[
CZ19
, Proposition 6.4]
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Assume that
K_{X}+D
is not nef and let
C
be an extremal rational curve for some
(K_{X}+D)
-extremal face
F
. Let
J\subset I
be the set of all
j\in I
for which
C\subset D_{j}
and let
U_{J}=D_{J}\backslash{\mathop{{\rm Supp\,}}}D^{J}
. Then for every closed point
x\in C\cap U_{J}
there exists a morphism
g:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to D_{J}
such that
x\in g({\mathbb{P}}^{1})
,
[g({\mathbb{P}}^{1})]\in F
and
g^{*}(D^{J})
is supported in at most one point.

Proof.

Let
f:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to C
be the normalization of
C
and let
V
be the connected component of

{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},D_{J})
containing
[f]
.
The
size
of a
0
-dimensional scheme
G
of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
is the maximum length of a subscheme contained in
G
, which is supported at a single point. Let
g:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to D_{J}
be a morphism with
[g]\in V^{x}
. Since
x\in C^{\prime}:=g({\mathbb{P}}^{1})
, we have
C^{\prime}\not\subset{\mathop{{\rm Supp\,}}}D^{J}
and
g^{*}(D^{J})
is a well defined Cartier divisor. Let us choose
g
for which
g^{*}(D^{J})
has the greatest size that we denote by
s
. We claim that
g
satisfies the required assertions.

Let
G\subset g^{*}(D^{J})
be a subscheme of length
s
supported at a single point.
Since it is clear that
[C^{\prime}]\in F
, we need only to check the last condition. Assume that
g^{*}D^{J}
is supported in
m\geq 2
points.
Let
W
be an irreducible component of
{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},D_{J};G\subset D^{J},0\to x)
containing
[g]
,
and let
\tilde{W}
be an irreducible component of
{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},D_{J})
containing
W
.
Since
D^{J}\cdot C^{\prime}\geq(m-1)+s
and
W
is an irreducible component of the fibre of
\tilde{W}\to D_{J}
,
h\to h(0)
, over
x
, we have

\displaystyle\dim W

\displaystyle\geq\dim\tilde{W}-\dim D_{J}\geq{\chi({\mathbb{P}}^{1},g^{*}T_{D_{J}})-\deg G-\dim D_{J}=-K_{D_{J}}\cdot C^{\prime}-s}

\displaystyle\geq{-(K_{D_{J}}+D^{J})\cdot C^{\prime}+m-1=-(K_{X}+D)\cdot C+m-1\geq 2}.

Note that
C^{\prime}
is an extremal rational curve, so for large
m\gg 0
it has minimal degree with respect to ample divisors of the form
-(K_{X}+D)+mH_{F}
. Hence we get a contradiction in the same way as in the proof of
[
KM99
, Corollary 5.4]
(or
[
CZ19
, Proposition 6.4]
).
∎

Remark 2.2
.

Note that
D_{J}
need not be irreducible although every connected component of
D_{J}
is smooth and irreducible.

2.2
Loci of rational curves on snc pairs

As pointed out in the proof of
[
KPSW00
, Proposition 2.9]
, the following proposition due to P. Ionescu and J. Wiśniewski follows from
[
Kol96
, Chapter IV, Proposition 2.5]
(note that unlike claimed in
[
Kol96
]
, the proof does not give any information if we do not assume that
V
is generically unsplit).

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety and let
C
be a rational curve in
X
. Let
f:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to C
be the normalization of
C
and let
V
be a closed irreducible subset of
{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X)
, which contains
[f]
and which is invariant under the action of
{\mathop{\operatorname{Aut}\,}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1})
.
If the family
V
is unsplit then for any closed point
x\in{\rm locus}(V)
we have

\dim V\leq\dim{\rm locus}(V)+\dim{\rm locus}(V,x)+1.

Let
(X,D)
be a projective snc pair with
D\neq 0
and let
U=X\backslash{\mathop{{\rm Supp\,}}}D
as before.
For any family
V\subset{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X)
we set
{\rm locus}_{U}(V):={\rm locus}(V)\cap U
and
{\rm locus}_{U}(V,x):={\rm locus}(V,x)\cap U
.

If
f:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to X
is a fixed morphism such that
f({\mathbb{P}}^{1})\not\subset{\mathop{{\rm Supp\,}}}D
then
we can consider
f^{*}D
as a collection of effective Cartier divisors on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. If
g
with
g({\mathbb{P}}^{1})\not\subset{\mathop{{\rm Supp\,}}}D
is in the same connected component of
{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X;f^{*}D\subset D)
as
f
then
f^{*}D_{i}=g^{*}D_{i}
for all
i\in I
. This follows from the fact that
\deg f^{*}D=\deg g^{*}D
and
f^{*}D_{i}\leq g^{*}D_{i}
for
i\in I
.

The following proposition is a logarithmic analogue of the above proposition.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
C
be a rational curve in
X
and let
f:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to C
be the normalization of
C
.
Assume that
f^{-1}(U)\simeq{\mathbb{A}}^{1}

and let
W
be a closed irreducible subset of
{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X;f^{*}D\subset D)
containing
[f]
and invariant under the action of the subgroup of
{\mathop{\operatorname{Aut}\,}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1})
fixing the support of
f^{*}D
. If the family
W
is unsplit then for any closed point
x\in{\rm locus}_{U}(W)
we have

\dim W\leq\dim{\rm locus}_{U}(W)+\dim{\rm locus}_{U}(W,x).

Proof.

Let
\tilde{W}={\mathop{\operatorname{Aut}\,}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1})\cdot W\subset{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X)
. Since
C
is not contained in the support of
D
,
W
is not
{\mathop{\operatorname{Aut}\,}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1})
-invariant. Hence

\dim\tilde{W}=\dim W+1
. Let us consider the map
F_{\tilde{W}}:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times\tilde{W}\to X
. For any
x\in{\rm locus}_{U}(W)
there exists
p\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and
[g]\in\tilde{W}
such that
g(p)=x
. Taking an automorphism
h
of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
such that
h(0)=p
, we see that
x=gh(0)
and
[gh]\in\tilde{W}
. So already the image of the map
\varphi:\tilde{W}\to X
,
[g]\to g(0)
contains
{\rm locus}_{U}(W)
.
In particular,
(\varphi^{-1}(x))_{red}=\{[g]\in\tilde{W}:g(0)=x\}=\tilde{W}^{x}
so
we have

\dim\tilde{W}^{x}\geq\dim\tilde{W}-\dim{\rm locus}_{U}(W).

Similarly, the image of the map
\psi:\tilde{W}^{x}\to X
,
[g]\to g(1),
contains
{\rm locus}_{U}(W,x)\backslash\{x\}
. So for any closed point
y\in{\rm locus}_{U}(W,x)\backslash\{x\}
we have

\dim\{[g]\in\tilde{W}:g(0)=x,g(1)=y\}\geq\dim\tilde{W}^{x}-\dim{\rm locus}_{U}(W,x).

If
\dim\{[g]\in\tilde{W}:g(0)=x,g(1)=y\}\geq 2
then
f
moves, fixing
0
and
1
, in a
2
-dimensional family and so its image must move. Since
W
is unsplit, the family
\tilde{W}
is also unsplit and we get a contradiction with
Mori’s Bend and Break (see
[
Kol96
, Chapter II, Corollary 5.5.2]
). Therefore

\dim\tilde{W}=\dim W+1\leq\dim{\rm locus}_{U}(W)+\dim\tilde{W}^{x}\leq\dim{\rm locus}_{U}(W)+\dim{\rm locus}_{U}(W,x)+1.

∎

Every vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
splits into a direct sum of the form
\bigoplus{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(a_{i})
. We set
{\mathop{\rm rk\,}}^{+}({\mathcal{E}}):=\#\{i:a_{i}>0\}
.

The following proposition is an analogue of
[
Kol96
, Chapter II, Proposition 3.10]
in the logarithmic case.

Proposition 2.5
.

Assume that for
f:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to X
, the divisor
f^{*}D
is supported in at most one point.
Let us fix a closed point
x\in U
and let
W
be an irreducible component of

{\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},X;f^{*}D\subset D)
containing
[f]
.
Then for any closed point
p\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\backslash(\{0\}\cup{\mathop{{\rm Supp\,}}}f^{*}D)

and any
g:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to X
such that
[g]\in W^{x}
we have

{\mathop{\rm rk\,}}dF_{W^{x}}(p,[g])={\mathop{\rm rk\,}}^{+}(g^{*}T_{X}(-\log D)).

In particular, we have

\dim{\rm locus}_{U}(W,x)\leq\max_{\{g:\,[g]\in W^{x}\}}{\mathop{\rm rk\,}}^{+}\left(g^{*}T_{X}(-\log D)\right).

Proof.

Let
I_{0}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}
be the ideal sheaf of
\{0\}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.
As in
[
Kol96
, II.3]
we have

T_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times W^{x}}\otimes k(p,[g])=T_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}\otimes k(p)+H^{0}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},g^{*}T_{X}(-\log D)\otimes I_{0})

and
dF_{W^{x}}(p,[g])=dg(p)+\phi(p,g)
, where
dg(p):T_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}\otimes k(p)\to g^{*}T_{X}\otimes k(p)=T_{X}\otimes k(g(p))

is the differential of
g
at
p
and

\phi(p,g):H^{0}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},g^{*}T_{X}(-\log D)\otimes I_{0})\to g^{*}T_{X}(-\log D)\otimes I_{0}\otimes k(p)=T_{X}\otimes k(g(p))

is the evaluation map. Let us set
B=(f^{*}D)_{red}
and recall that
g^{*}D=f^{*}D
. A standard local computation shows that the differential of
g
induces the map
T_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(-B)=T_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(-\log B)\to g^{*}T_{X}(-\log D)
. So the first claim follows from surjectivity of the evaluation map

H^{0}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},T_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(-B)\otimes I_{0})\to T_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(-B)\otimes I_{0}\otimes k(p)=T_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}\otimes k(p).

The second part follows from
[
Har77
, Chapter III, Proposition 10.6]
.
∎

3
Symplectic and contact structures on smooth Lie algebroids

In this section we revise symplectic and contact structures in the Lie algeborid setup. We also recall and revise Atiyah extensions in the log case and prove some auxiliary results.

3.1
Lie algebroids

Let
X
be a
k
-variety and let

{\mathbb{L}}=(L,[\cdot,\cdot]_{L},\alpha:L\to T_{X})
be a
k
-Lie algebroid on
X
such that
L
is a vector bundle (in this case we say that
{\mathbb{L}}
is
smooth
). Let us recall that
{\mathbb{L}}
is given by a sheaf of
k
-Lie algebras structure on
L

and the anchor map
\alpha
, which is a map of both
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules and sheaves of
k
-Lie algebras, and which satisfies Leibniz’s rule

[x,fy]=(\alpha(x))(f)y+f[x,y]

for all local sections
f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
and
x,y\in L
(see, e.g.,
[
Lan14
, Definition 2.1]
).
In the following we usually omit
k
in the notation and often write
{\mathbb{L}}
not only for a Lie algebroid but also for the underlying vector bundle
L
.

For any Lie algebroid
{\mathbb{L}}
we can consider the canonical de Rham complex

(\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\bullet},d^{\bullet}_{{\mathbb{L}}})
, where
\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{m}=\bigwedge^{m}(L^{*})
(see
[
Lan14
, 2.1]
). The differential in this complex is induced by

d_{{\mathbb{L}}}\in{\rm Der}_{k}({\mathcal{O}}_{X},\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1})={\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}_{X}(\Omega_{X}^{1},\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}),

corresponding to the dual of the anchor map
\alpha:L\to T_{X}
. Note that the de Rham complex is a complex of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules but the maps in this complex are only
k
-linear.

Let us recall that a map of Lie algebroids is an
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-linear map of underlying vector bundles that commutes with anchors and Lie brackets.
It is useful to use the following definition that is modelled on the Lie algebra case:

Definition 3.1
.

An
abelian extension
of a Lie algebroid
{\mathbb{L}}
by
{\mathbb{L}}^{\prime\prime}
is a short exact sequence of Lie algebroids

0\to{\mathbb{L}}^{\prime\prime}\to{\mathbb{L}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbb{L}}\to 0

in which
{\mathbb{L}}^{\prime\prime}
is a trivial Lie algebroid on a trivial bundle.
An abelian extension is called
central
if
{\mathbb{L}}^{\prime\prime}
is generated by global sections whose images belong to the center of the Lie algebra
H^{0}(X,{\mathbb{L}}^{\prime})
.

Both abelian and central extensions appear naturally in many places in this paper. Note that the word “abelian” (or “central”) can be somewhat misleading as it does not mean that
{\mathbb{L}}^{\prime\prime}
is abelian (central) in
{\mathbb{L}}^{\prime}
.

3.2
Symplectic structure

Let us fix a
k
-variety
X
equipped with a smooth Lie algebroid
{\mathbb{L}}
. Let
{\mathcal{L}}
be
a line bundle on
X
.
We say that a
k
-linear morphism
\nabla:{\mathcal{L}}\to\Omega^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}}
is an
{\mathbb{L}}
-connection
if it satisfies the Leibniz rule

\nabla(fe)=f\nabla(e)+d_{{\mathbb{L}}}(f)\otimes e

for
e\in{\mathcal{L}}
and
f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
. Then we can define
k
-linear maps
\nabla_{i}:\Omega^{i}_{{\mathbb{L}}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}}\to\Omega^{i+1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}}
by setting

\nabla_{i}(\omega\otimes e)=d_{{\mathbb{L}}}(\omega)\otimes e+(-1)^{i}\omega\wedge\nabla(e)

for
e\in{\mathcal{L}}
and
\omega\in\Omega^{i}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
. If
\nabla_{1}\circ\nabla=0
then we say that
\nabla
is
flat
. If
\nabla
is flat then the associated sequence

{\mathcal{L}}\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\nabla}\Omega^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}}\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\nabla_{1}}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathbb{L}}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}}\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\nabla_{2}}\cdots

is a complex. By abuse of notation in the following we write
\nabla
instead of
\nabla_{i}
.

Let us also recall that for a line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
we have a canonical bijection between flat
{\mathbb{L}}
-connections on
{\mathcal{L}}
, and
{\mathbb{L}}
-module structures on
{\mathcal{L}}
compatible with the
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module structure (see, e.g.,
[
Lan14
, Lemma 3.2]
).

Definition 3.2
.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}
be a line bundle equipped with a flat
{\mathbb{L}}
-connection
\nabla
.
An
{\mathcal{L}}
-valued symplectic form
on
{\mathbb{L}}
is a section
\omega\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{2}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}})
such that

1.

\omega
is
closed
, i.e.,
\nabla(\omega)=0
,

2.

\omega
is
nondegenerate
, i.e., the map

\hat{\omega}:{{\mathbb{L}}}\to\Omega^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}}={\mathop{{\mathcal{H}}om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}({\mathbb{L}},{\mathcal{L}})
,
x\to\omega(x\wedge\cdot)
is an isomorphism.

The second condition in the above definition implies that the rank of
L
is even. If
{\mathop{\rm rk\,}}L=2r
then

\omega^{\wedge r}
induces an isomorphism

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\Omega^{2r}_{{\mathbb{L}}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}}^{\otimes r}.

The above definition generalizes holomorphic symplectic manifolds that correspond to smooth varieties with an
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-valued symplectic form on the canonical Lie algebroid structure on the tangent bundle, where
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
is equipped with the canonical connection
d:{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to\Omega_{X}^{1}
.

Let
(X,D)
be an snc pair. The logarithmic tangent bundle
T_{X}(-\log D)
carries a canonical Lie algebroid structure with canonical inclusion
\alpha:T_{X}(-\log D)\to T_{X}
as the anchor map and with the Lie bracket induced from the Lie bracket on
T_{X}=Der_{k}({\mathcal{O}}_{X},{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
. By abuse of notation we denote this Lie algebroid again by
T_{X}(-\log D)
and we call it the canonical Lie algebroid.
The following definition is a reformulation of the standard definition (see, e.g.,
[
Got02
, Definition 1.1]
).

Definition 3.3
.

A
log symplectic form
on
(X,D)
is an
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-valued symplectic form on the canonical Lie algebroid
T_{X}(-\log D)
, where
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
is equipped with the canonical logarithmic connection
d:{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D)
. A pair
(X,D)
is called
log symplectic
if it admits a log symplectic form.

3.3
Contact structure

Let us fix a
k
-variety
X
equipped with a smooth Lie algebroid
{\mathbb{L}}
.
Let
{\mathcal{L}}
be a line bundle on
X
and let
\theta:L\to{\mathcal{L}}
be an
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-linear map with kernel
{\mathcal{F}}
.
Although the Lie bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]_{{\mathbb{L}}}:{\bigwedge}^{2}_{k}L\to L
is not
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-linear, the composition

{\bigwedge}^{2}{\mathcal{F}}\longrightarrow{\bigwedge}^{2}_{k}L\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{[\cdot,\cdot]_{{\mathbb{L}}}}L\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\theta}{\mathcal{L}},\quad x\wedge y\to\theta([x,y]_{{\mathbb{L}}})

is
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-linear (see, e.g.,
[
Lan14
, Lemma 4.4]
).

Definition 3.4
.

An
{\mathcal{L}}
-valued contact structure
on
{\mathbb{L}}
is a surjective
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-linear map
\theta:L\to{\mathcal{L}}

such that
{\bigwedge}^{2}{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{L}},

x\wedge y\to\theta([x,y]_{{\mathbb{L}}})
is nondegenerate, i.e., the map
\hat{\theta}:{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{F}}^{*}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}}={\mathop{{\mathcal{H}}om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}({\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{L}})
,
x\to\theta([x,\cdot]_{{\mathbb{L}}})
is an isomorphism.

Note that our definition implies that
{\mathop{\rm rk\,}}{\mathcal{F}}\geq 2
.
Since
\hat{\theta}
is a non-degenerate skew-symmetric form on
{\mathcal{F}}
with values in
{\mathcal{L}}
(i.e.,
\hat{\theta}=-\hat{\theta}^{*}\otimes\mathop{\rm Id}_{{\mathcal{L}}}
), the rank of
{\mathcal{F}}
is even and
{\bigwedge}^{2}{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{L}}
induces an isomorphism
\det{\mathcal{F}}={\bigwedge}^{2r}{\mathcal{F}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{\otimes r}
, where
2r
is the rank of
{\mathcal{F}}
. In particular,
\det L\simeq{\mathcal{L}}^{r+1}
. Since
{\mathcal{F}}\subset L
is not closed under the Lie bracket on
L
, there is no natural Lie algebroid structure on
{\mathcal{F}}
and we cannot say that
\hat{\theta}
is a symplectic form. This problem will be resolved in Proposition
3.9
, where we show that there exists a natural symplectic form compatible with
\hat{\theta}
on some other Lie algebroid.

Lemma 3.5
.

For any
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-linear map
\omega:{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\to\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}
with cokernel being a vector bundle
{\mathcal{F}}^{*}
, the composition

\varphi:{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\omega}\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{d_{{\mathbb{L}}}}\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{2}\to{\bigwedge}^{2}({\mathcal{F}}^{*})=({\bigwedge}^{2}{\mathcal{F}})^{*}

is
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-linear. If
\omega=\theta^{*}
then up to a sign
\varphi
coincides with the dual to
{\bigwedge}^{2}{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{L}},

x\wedge y\to\theta([x,y]_{L})
.

Proof.

To prove that
\varphi
is
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-linear, we must show that for any local section
s\in{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}
and any local function
f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
we have

\varphi(fs)=f\varphi(s).

Let
\eta=\omega(s)\in\Omega^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
. Let us evaluate the 2-form
\varphi(fs)
on two local sections
x,y\in{\mathcal{F}}
, using the standard formula for the Lie algebroid exterior derivative and the Leibniz rule:

\displaystyle d_{{\mathbb{L}}}(f\eta)(x\wedge y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{x}((f\eta)(y))-\alpha_{y}((f\eta)(x))-(f\eta)([x,y]_{{\mathbb{L}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\alpha_{x}(f)\eta(y)+f\alpha_{x}(\eta(y))\big)-\big(\alpha_{y}(f)\eta(x)+f\alpha_{y}(\eta(x))\big)-f\eta([x,y]_{{\mathbb{L}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f\cdot d_{{\mathbb{L}}}\eta(x\wedge y)+\alpha_{x}(f)\eta(y)-\alpha_{y}(f)\eta(x).

Because
{\mathcal{F}}^{*}
is the cokernel of
\omega
, the 1-form
\eta=\omega(s)
vanishes on any section of
{\mathcal{F}}
.
Therefore
\eta(x)=\eta(y)=0
and we get

d_{{\mathbb{L}}}(f\eta)(x\wedge y)=f\cdot d_{{\mathbb{L}}}\eta(x\wedge y).

To see the second assertion note that for any
x,y\in{\mathcal{F}}
,
s\in{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}

and
\eta=\omega(s)\in\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}
we have

(d_{{\mathbb{L}}}\eta)(x\wedge y)=\alpha_{x}(\eta(y))-\alpha_{y}(\eta(x))-\eta([x,y]_{{{\mathbb{L}}}})=-\eta([x,y]_{{{\mathbb{L}}}}).

∎

The above lemma allows us to consider

\varphi^{\wedge r}:{\mathcal{L}}^{-r}\to{\bigwedge}^{2r}({\mathcal{F}}^{*})=\det({\mathcal{F}}^{*})=\det\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}.

If
\omega=\theta^{*}
then
\theta
is nondegenerate if and only if the map
\varphi^{\wedge r}
is an isomorphism.
Let us also note that although
d_{{\mathbb{L}}}\omega:{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\to\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{2}
is not
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-linear,

\omega\wedge(d_{{\mathbb{L}}}\omega)^{\wedge r}
gives a well defined
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-linear map
from
{\mathcal{L}}^{-(r+1)}
to
\det\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}
. This map can be identified with
\varphi^{\wedge r}\otimes\mathop{\rm Id}_{{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}}
. A standard computation proving these assertions is left to the reader.

Definition 3.6
.

An
{\mathcal{L}}
-valued form
\omega\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}})={\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}\,({\mathcal{L}}^{-1},\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1})
is called
contact
if the induced
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-linear map
\omega\wedge(d_{{\mathbb{L}}}\omega)^{\wedge r}:{\mathcal{L}}^{-(r+1)}\to\det\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}
is an isomorphism.

Note that the above remarks show that giving an
{\mathcal{L}}
-valued contact structure on
{\mathbb{L}}
is equivalent to giving an

{\mathcal{L}}
-valued contact form.

Sometimes it is also convenient to consider contact structures defined only at the generic point of
X
:

Definition 3.7
.

A
generically
{\mathcal{L}}
-valued contact structure
on
{\mathbb{L}}
is a non-zero
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-linear map
\theta:{\mathbb{L}}\to{\mathcal{L}}
for which there exists a non-empty Zariski open subset
U\subset X
such that
\theta|_{U}
is an
{\mathcal{L}}|_{U}
-valued contact structure on
{\mathbb{L}}|_{U}
.

If
\theta:{\mathbb{L}}\to{\mathcal{L}}
is a generically
{\mathcal{L}}
-valued contact structure and
\omega:{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\to\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}
is its dual then
\omega\wedge(d\omega)^{\wedge r}
defines a non-zero section of
\det\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}^{r+1}
. In particular,
\det\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}={\mathcal{L}}^{-(r+1)}(B)
for some effective divisor
B
.
\omega
defines an
{\mathcal{L}}
-valued contact structure on
{\mathbb{L}}
if and only if
B=0
.

3.4
Jet bundles and the Atiyah extension for Lie algebroids

Let us recall Atiyah classes defined by Lie algebroids after
[
CVdB10
, 4.2.5 and 8.1]
.
If
{\mathbb{L}}
is a Lie algebroid then we define the
first
{\mathbb{L}}
-jet
k
-algebra bundle

J^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
as

{\mathop{{\mathcal{H}}om}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}({L}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{X},{\mathcal{O}}_{X})=\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
with a natural commutative
k
-algebra structure given by
(\eta_{1},f_{1})\cdot(\eta_{2},f_{2})=(f_{2}\eta_{1}+f_{1}\eta_{2},f_{1}f_{2})
.
J^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
comes with
an
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-bimodule structure, where the left
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module structure is
given by the monomorphism

\alpha_{1}:{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to J^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}},\quad f\to(0,f)

and the right
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module structure is
given by the monomorphism

\alpha_{2}:{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to J^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}},\quad f\to(d_{{\mathbb{L}}}(f),f).

Since
\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}
is an ideal in
J^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
, we have the short exact sequence

0\to\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}\longrightarrow J^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to 0

of
J^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
-modules. In general, this sequence is not split although it is split both as a sequence of left
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules and as a sequence of right
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules.

For a vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}
on
X
, we define its
first
{\mathbb{L}}
-jet bundle
as
J^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{E}})=J^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{E}}
. It has a natural structure of a left
J^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
-module and the above short exact sequence induces the short exact sequence

0\to{\mathcal{E}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}\simeq\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{E}}\longrightarrow J^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{E}})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}\to 0

of left
J^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
-modules (hence also of left
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules).
This sequence is called the
{\mathbb{L}}
-Atiyah extension
of
{\mathcal{E}}
and it
defines so called
{\mathbb{L}}
-Atiyah class
of
{\mathcal{E}}
:

\alpha_{{\mathcal{E}}}\in{\mathop{{\rm Ext\,}}}^{1}_{X}({\mathcal{E}},\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{E}})=H^{1}(X,\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathop{{{\mathcal{E}}nd\,}}}{\mathcal{E}}).

This sequence is split as a sequence of sheaves of abelian groups (or even of sheaves of
k
-vector spaces) with the canonical splitting
j^{1}:{\mathcal{E}}\to J^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{E}})

induced by
\alpha_{2}
. Existence of an
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-linear splitting of this sequence (treated as a sequence of left
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules) is equivalent to existence of an
{\mathbb{L}}
-connection
{\mathcal{E}}\to\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{E}}
.

If
{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{L}}
is a line bundle, tensoring the
{\mathbb{L}}
-Atiyah extension of
{\mathcal{L}}
by
{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}
(on the left) gives the extension

0\to\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{L}})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to 0

in which
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{L}})={\mathcal{L}}^{-1}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}J^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{L}})
. Sometimes we also call this extension the
{\mathbb{L}}
-Atiyah extension of
{\mathcal{L}}
.
By construction we have a canonical pushout diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega_{X}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}_{T_{X}}({\mathcal{L}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{L}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

in which
\alpha^{*}
is the dual of the anchor map
\alpha:L\to T_{X}
.
The
T_{X}
-Atiyah class of
{\mathcal{L}}
can be identified with the first Chern class
c_{1}({\mathcal{L}})\in H^{1}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1})

(sometimes people use a different normalization in the complex case).
The
{\mathbb{L}}
-Atiyah class is therefore the image of
c_{1}({\mathcal{L}})
under the canonical map
H^{1}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1})\to H^{1}(X,\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1})={\mathop{{\rm Ext\,}}}^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X},\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1})
induced by
d_{{\mathbb{L}}}
. We denote the corresponding class by
c_{1}^{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{L}})
.

In the following we need also a generalization of the Atiyah extension for a collection
\underline{{\mathcal{L}}}=({\mathcal{L}}_{1},...,{\mathcal{L}}_{s})
of line bundles on
X
. Then we define the
multi-
{\mathbb{L}}
-Atiyah extension of
\underline{{\mathcal{L}}}

0\to\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\underline{{\mathcal{L}}}})\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{\oplus s}\to 0

as the pushout of the direct sum of Atiyah extensions for line bundles
{\mathcal{L}}_{1},...,{\mathcal{L}}_{s}

0\to(\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1})^{\oplus s}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{1})\oplus...\oplus{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{s})\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{\oplus s}\to 0

by the codiagonal map
(\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1})^{\oplus s}\to\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}
.
Clearly, the extension class of the multi-Atiyah extension in
{\mathop{{\rm Ext\,}}}^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{\oplus s},\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1})=H^{1}(X,\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1})^{\oplus s}
is given by
\oplus_{i=1}^{s}c_{1}^{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{i})
.

3.5
Differential operators for Lie algebroids

Let
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
be the universal enveloping algebra of the Lie algebroid
{\mathbb{L}}
and let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a vector bundle on
X
.
Then we define the sheaf
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{E}})
of
{\mathbb{L}}
-differential operators acting on
{\mathcal{E}}^{*}
as a subsheaf of
{\mathop{{{\mathcal{E}}nd\,}}}_{k}({\mathcal{E}}^{*})
equal to
\bigcup_{i\geq 0}{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq i}({\mathcal{E}})
, where

{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq-1}({\mathcal{E}}):=0
and

{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq i}({\mathcal{E}}):=\{x\in{\mathop{{{\mathcal{E}}nd\,}}}_{k}({\mathcal{E}}^{*}):\,xf-fx\in{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq i-1}({\mathcal{E}})\hbox{ for all }f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\}

for
i\geq 0
.
Then
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})={\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
and we have a canonical isomorphism of sheaves of rings

{\mathcal{E}}^{*}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{E}}\simeq{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{E}})

given by

(s\otimes x\otimes u)(t)=x(\langle u,t\rangle)s,

where
s,t\in{\mathcal{E}}^{*}
,
u\in{\mathcal{E}}
and
x\in{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}
.

Note that
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{E}})
is a sheaf of rings with the ring structure induced from

{\mathop{{{\mathcal{E}}nd\,}}}_{k}({\mathcal{E}}^{*})
and
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 0}({\mathcal{E}})\simeq{\mathop{{{\mathcal{E}}nd\,}}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}({\mathcal{E}}^{*})
is a sheaf of subrings of
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{E}})
containing
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
.

If
{\mathcal{L}}
is a line bundle then
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 0}({\mathcal{L}})\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
and

{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})
has a canonical Lie algebroid structure, defined by the Lie bracket coming from the associative algebra structure on
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{L}})

and the anchor map

{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})/{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 0}({\mathcal{L}})\simeq{\mathbb{L}}\to T_{X}.

Moreover, we have a central extension of Lie algebroids

0\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})\longrightarrow{\mathbb{L}}\to 0,

since the image of
1\in H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
is central, acting as the identity on
{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}
. This extension can be identified with the dual to the
{\mathbb{L}}
-Atiyah extension of
{\mathcal{L}}
and its extension class in
{\mathop{{\rm Ext\,}}}^{1}({\mathbb{L}},{\mathcal{O}}_{X})=H^{1}(X,\Omega^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}})
is given by
-c_{1}^{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{L}})
.
In particular, we have
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})=({\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{L}}))^{*}
,
i.e.,
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{L}})=\Omega_{{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})}^{1}
.

If
\underline{{\mathcal{L}}}=({\mathcal{L}}_{1},...,{\mathcal{L}}_{s})
is a collection of line bundles then we can construct

0\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{\oplus s}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}(\underline{{\mathcal{L}}})\longrightarrow{\mathbb{L}}\to 0

as the pullback of the direct sum of

0\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{\oplus s}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({{\mathcal{L}}_{1}})\oplus...\oplus{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({{\mathcal{L}}_{s}})\longrightarrow{\mathbb{L}}^{\oplus s}\to 0

by the diagonal map
{\mathbb{L}}\to{\mathbb{L}}^{\oplus s}
. The class of this extension is given by
-\oplus_{i=1}^{s}c_{1}^{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{i})
.
Note that
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}(\underline{{\mathcal{L}}})
has a natural structure of a Lie algebroid for which the above extension is a central extension of Lie algebroids. Under the identification
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}(\underline{{\mathcal{L}}})=({\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\underline{{\mathcal{L}}}}))^{*}
, the anchor map corresponds to the dual of

\Omega_{X}^{1}\to\Omega_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{1}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\underline{{\mathcal{L}}}})
.

3.6
Relation between contact and symplectic structures

Below we describe generalization of the classical process of symplectification of a contact manifold (see, e.g.,
[
Arn89
, Appendix 4 E]
) to arbitrary contact structures on Lie algebroids.
We follow the approach of
[
LeB95
, Proposition 2.4]
and
[
Bea98
, Lemma 1.4]

(see also
[
KPSW00
, 2.1 and 2.2]
). Classically this gives a relation between contact structures on a variety and
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
-invariant symplectic structures on the associated principal
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
-bundle. The Lie algebroid point of view makes this a relation between contact and symplectic structures on the same variety but for different Lie algebroids.

First, let us make the following easy observation.

Lemma 3.8
.

A line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
has a canonical flat
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})
-connection
\nabla^{\sf can}
.

Proof.

By construction we have a canonical map

j^{1}:{\mathcal{L}}\to J^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{L}})\simeq{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}({\mathcal{L}})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}}=\Omega_{{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})}^{1}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}},

giving a
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})
-connection
\nabla^{\sf can}
. The connection is flat as
{\mathcal{L}}
is a line bundle.
Alternatively, we can obtain the required connection
\nabla^{\sf can}
considering the action of
{\mathbb{L}}
-differential operators on
{\mathcal{L}}
, dual to the canonical action on
{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}
. ∎

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
{\mathbb{L}}
be a (smooth)
k
-Lie algebroid on a
k
-variety
X
and let
{\mathcal{L}}
be a fixed line bundle on
X
. There exists a natural bijection between
{\mathcal{L}}
-valued contact structures on
{\mathbb{L}}
and
{\mathcal{L}}
-valued symplectic forms on the Lie algebroid
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})
, where
{\mathcal{L}}
is equipped with the canonical flat
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})
-connection
\nabla^{\sf can}
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathbb{M}}:={\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})
and let
\pi:{\mathbb{M}}\to{\mathbb{L}}
be the
canonical map. Let

e=\mathrm{id}_{{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}}\in H^{0}(X,{\mathbb{M}})

be the canonical section spanning
\ker(\pi)\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
. The section

e
is central in
{\mathbb{M}}
and acts as the identity on
{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}
.
Consequently, it acts as minus the identity on
{\mathcal{L}}
.
Therefore, for every
\omega\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{{\mathbb{M}}}^{j}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}}),

the covariant Lie derivative along
e
satisfies

\mathbf{L}_{e}\omega=\nabla^{\sf can}_{e}\circ\omega-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\omega(\dots,[e,\cdot]_{{\mathbb{M}}},\dots)=-\omega.

So Cartan’s magic formula

\mathbf{L}_{e}=\nabla^{\sf can}\circ\iota_{e}+\iota_{e}\circ\nabla^{\sf can}
implies that

-\omega=\nabla^{\sf can}(\iota_{e}\omega)+\iota_{e}(\nabla^{\sf can}\omega)

(1)

for all
\omega\in H^{0}(X,\Omega^{j}_{{\mathbb{M}}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}})
.

Step 1.

Let
\omega\in H^{0}(X,\Omega^{2}_{{\mathbb{M}}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}})={\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}(\bigwedge^{2}{\mathbb{M}},{\mathcal{L}})
be an
{\mathcal{L}}
-valued symplectic form on
{\mathbb{M}}
. Because
\nabla^{\sf can}\omega=0
, equation (
1
) reduces to

\omega=\nabla^{\sf can}\tilde{\theta},

where
\tilde{\theta}=-\iota_{e}\omega\in H^{0}(X,\Omega^{1}_{{\mathbb{M}}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}})={\mathop{{\rm Hom}}}({\mathbb{M}},{\mathcal{L}}).

Skew-symmetry implies
\tilde{\theta}(e)=-\omega(e,e)=0
, so
\tilde{\theta}
vanishes on
\ker(\pi)
. Thus
\tilde{\theta}
uniquely descends to a section of
\Omega^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}}
, representing an
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-linear map
\theta:{\mathbb{L}}\to{\mathcal{L}}
. Nondegeneracy of
\omega
implies
\tilde{\theta}_{p}\neq 0
at every point
p\in X
, ensuring that
\theta
is surjective.
Let
{\mathcal{F}}:=\ker(\theta)\subset{\mathbb{L}}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}:=\pi^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})=\ker(\tilde{\theta})\subset{\mathbb{M}}
. For any
x,y\in{\mathcal{F}}
with lifts
\tilde{x},\tilde{y}\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
we have

\omega(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=(\nabla^{\sf can}\tilde{\theta})(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=\nabla^{\sf can}_{\tilde{x}}(\tilde{\theta}(\tilde{y}))-\nabla^{\sf can}_{\tilde{y}}(\tilde{\theta}(\tilde{x}))-\tilde{\theta}([\tilde{x},\tilde{y}]_{{\mathbb{M}}})=-\theta([x,y]_{{\mathbb{L}}}).

Because
\omega(e,\tilde{\mathcal{F}})=\tilde{\theta}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}})=0
, the radical of
\omega|_{\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}
is precisely
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}e
. Nondegeneracy of
\omega
on
{\mathbb{M}}
forces the induced bilinear form
\omega|_{\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}
to be nondegenerate on
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}/({\mathcal{O}}_{X}\cdot e)\cong{\mathcal{F}}
. Thus,
x\wedge y\mapsto\theta([x,y]_{{\mathbb{L}}})
is nondegenerate on
{\mathcal{F}}
, making
\theta
a contact structure.

Step 2.

Given an
{\mathcal{L}}
-valued contact structure
\theta:{\mathbb{L}}\to{\mathcal{L}}
, set
\tilde{\theta}=\pi^{*}\theta^{*}=\theta\circ\pi\in H^{0}(X,\Omega^{1}_{{\mathbb{M}}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}})
and define

\omega:=\nabla^{\sf can}\tilde{\theta}\in H^{0}(X,\Omega^{2}_{{\mathbb{M}}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\mathcal{L}})

Flatness of
\nabla^{\sf can}
implies that
\nabla^{\sf can}\omega=0
,
so
\omega
is closed.

To prove that
\omega
is nondegenerate, let
{\mathcal{F}}:=\ker(\theta)
and
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}:=\pi^{-1}({\mathcal{F}})=\ker(\tilde{\theta})
.
Since
e
is central in
{\mathbb{M}}
,
\tilde{\theta}(e)=0
, and

\nabla^{\sf can}_{e}
acts as minus the identity on
{\mathcal{L}}
, we have, for
every local section
v
of
{\mathbb{M}}
,

\omega(e,v)=\nabla^{\sf can}_{e}\bigl(\tilde{\theta}(v)\bigr)-\nabla^{\sf can}_{v}\bigl(\tilde{\theta}(e)\bigr)-\tilde{\theta}([e,v]_{{\mathbb{M}}})=-\tilde{\theta}(v).

Suppose that a local section
v
of
{\mathbb{M}}
belongs to the radical of

\omega
. Pairing
v
with
e
, we obtain

0=\omega(e,v)=-\tilde{\theta}(v),

and hence
v\in\tilde{{\mathcal{F}}}
. So for every local section

w\in\tilde{{\mathcal{F}}}
we have

0=\omega(v,w)=\nabla^{\sf can}_{v}\bigl(\tilde{\theta}(w)\bigr)-\nabla^{\sf can}_{w}\bigl(\tilde{\theta}(v)\bigr)-\tilde{\theta}([v,w]_{{\mathbb{M}}})=-\theta\bigl([\pi(v),\pi(w)]_{{\mathbb{L}}}\bigr).

Since
\pi:\tilde{{\mathcal{F}}}\to{\mathcal{F}}
is surjective and the pairing

{\mathcal{F}}\times{\mathcal{F}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{L}},

(x,y)\longmapsto\theta([x,y]_{{\mathbb{L}}}),

is nondegenerate, it follows that
\pi(v)=0
. Consequently,

v=fe
for some local function
f
.

Finally, for every local section
w
of
{\mathbb{M}}
,

0=\omega(v,w)=f\,\omega(e,w)=-f\,\widetilde{\theta}(w).

The map
\tilde{\theta}=\theta\circ\pi:{\mathbb{M}}\to{\mathcal{L}}
is surjective, so
locally one can choose
w
such that
\tilde{\theta}(w)
generates

{\mathcal{L}}
. Hence
f=0
, and therefore
v=0
. Thus
\omega
is
nondegenerate and hence it is an
{\mathcal{L}}
-valued symplectic form on
{\mathbb{M}}
.

Step 3.

Let us check that the two constructions are mutually inverse. If

\tilde{\theta}=\pi^{*}\theta^{*}=\theta\circ\pi
, then

\iota_{e}\tilde{\theta}=0
and
\mathbf{L}_{e}\tilde{\theta}=-\tilde{\theta}
. Hence Cartan’s formula gives

-\iota_{e}\bigl(\nabla^{\sf can}\widetilde{\theta}\bigr)=-\mathbf{L}_{e}\widetilde{\theta}+\nabla^{\sf can}(\iota_{e}\widetilde{\theta})=\widetilde{\theta}.

Conversely, if
\omega
is closed, equation (
1
) gives

\nabla^{\sf can}(-\iota_{e}\omega)=\omega.

Thus the mutually inverse correspondences are determined by

\theta\circ\pi=-\iota_{e}\omega,\qquad\omega=\nabla^{\sf can}(\pi^{*}\theta^{*}).

∎

Remark 3.10
.

As in the proof of
[
LeB95
, Proposition 2.4]
we have the following
commutative diagram:

\textstyle{{\mathcal{F}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{\omega}_{{\mathcal{L}}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{F}}^{*}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega^{1}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}}

This diagram shows that the induced map
\Omega^{1}_{{\mathbb{L}}}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}\to{\mathbb{L}}
(using inverses of the isomorphisms) is compatible with both contact form on
{\mathbb{L}}
and symplectic form on
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})
.

A relation between the above point of view and the classical one (see, e.g.,
[
Bea98
, Lemma 1.4]
) can be explained as follows. Let

\pi:\widetilde{X}:={\mathop{{\rm Spec\,}}}_{X}(\bigoplus_{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{m})\to X

be the principal
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-bundle associated with
{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}
, and
let
\alpha:{\mathbb{L}}\longrightarrow T_{X}
denote the anchor map. Let us recall that one defines the
the inverse-image Lie algebroid
as

\pi^{!}{\mathbb{L}}:=T_{\widetilde{X}}\times_{\pi^{*}T_{X}}\pi^{*}{\mathbb{L}}=\left\{(v,\ell)\in T_{\widetilde{X}}\oplus\pi^{*}{\mathbb{L}}\ \middle|\ d\pi(v)=\pi^{*}\alpha(\ell)\right\}.

The anchor map is given by the first projection

\tilde{\alpha}:\pi^{!}{\mathbb{L}}\longrightarrow T_{\tilde{X}}
,

(v,\ell)\longmapsto v
.
To describe the Lie bracket, write two local sections of
\pi^{!}{\mathbb{L}}
as

u=\left(v,\sum_{i}f_{i}\pi^{*}\ell_{i}\right),\qquad u^{\prime}=\left(w,\sum_{j}g_{j}\pi^{*}m_{j}\right),

where
\ell_{i},m_{j}
are local sections of
{\mathbb{L}}
.
Then we define the Lie bracket on
\pi^{!}{\mathbb{L}}
by

[u,u^{\prime}]_{\pi^{!}{\mathbb{L}}}:=\Bigg([v,w],\,\sum_{i,j}f_{i}g_{j}\pi^{*}[\ell_{i},m_{j}]_{{\mathbb{L}}}\\
+\sum_{j}v(g_{j})\pi^{*}m_{j}-\sum_{i}w(f_{i})\pi^{*}\ell_{i}\Bigg).

This definition is independent of the chosen presentations of the
local sections.

Since
\pi
is smooth, there is a canonical short exact sequence of vector bundles

0\longrightarrow T_{\tilde{X}/X}\longrightarrow\pi^{!}{\mathbb{L}}\longrightarrow\pi^{*}{\mathbb{L}}\longrightarrow 0.

For a principal
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-bundle, the relative tangent bundle is
canonically trivialized by the Euler vector field
\varepsilon
.
Consequently, the above sequence can be written as

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{X}}\longrightarrow\pi^{!}{\mathbb{L}}\longrightarrow\pi^{*}{\mathbb{L}}\longrightarrow 0,

where the first non-zero map maps
1
to
-\epsilon
.
Since
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})
acts on
{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}
, every local section

v
of
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})
induces a

\mathbb{G}_{m}
-invariant vector field
\tilde{v}
on
\tilde{X}
.
This gives a canonical isomorphism

\Phi:\pi^{*}{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\simeq}\pi^{!}{\mathbb{L}},\qquad\pi^{*}v\longmapsto\bigl(\tilde{v},\pi^{*}\sigma(v)\bigr),

where
\sigma:{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})\longrightarrow{\mathbb{L}}
is the canonical projection.
The pullback of the canonical inclusion
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})
coincides with

{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{X}}\to\pi^{!}{\mathbb{L}}
mapping
1
to
-\epsilon
.

The canonical flat
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})
-connection on
{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}
pulls back, through

\Phi
, to a flat
\pi^{!}{\mathbb{L}}
-connection on
\pi^{*}{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}
. By the projection formula
\pi_{*}(\pi^{*}{\mathcal{L}}^{-1})\simeq\bigoplus_{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{m}
, so
\pi^{*}{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}
has a canonical section corresponding to
1\in H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{L}}^{0}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
. This section trivializes
\pi^{*}{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}
and it
is flat for the pullback connection. Consequently,
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{X}}
has a canonical flat
\pi^{!}{\mathbb{L}}
-connection corresponding to the pullback of
\nabla^{\sf can}
. In the following a
symplectic form on
\pi^{!}{\mathbb{L}}
is
an
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{X}}
-valued symplectic form, where
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{X}}
is equipped with this
\pi^{!}{\mathbb{L}}
-connection.
Such form is called
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
-equivariant
if it is homogeneous of weight
1
(cf.
[
Bea98
, 1.3]
).

Corollary 3.11
.

There exists a natural bijection between
{\mathcal{L}}
-valued contact structures on
{\mathbb{L}}
and
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
-equivariant
symplectic forms on the Lie algebroid
\pi^{!}{\mathbb{L}}
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to note that
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
-equivariant symplectic forms on the Lie algebroid
\pi^{!}{\mathbb{L}}

correspond to
{\mathcal{L}}
-valued symplectic forms on the Lie algebroid
{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathbb{L}}}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}})
and use Proposition
3.9
.
∎

Corollary 3.12
.

Let
(X,D)
be an snc pair. Let
{\mathcal{L}}
be a line bundle on
X
and let
\pi:\widetilde{X}\to X
be the principal
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-bundle associated with
{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}
. Let us set
{\mathbb{L}}:={\mathcal{D}}_{T_{X}(-\log D)}^{\leq 1}({\mathcal{L}}).

Then we have canonical bijections between:

1.

{\mathcal{L}}
-valued contact structures on
(X,D)
,

2.

{\mathcal{L}}
-valued symplectic forms on
{\mathbb{L}}
, where
{\mathcal{L}}
is equipped with the canonical flat
{{\mathbb{L}}}
-connection,

3.

{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
-equivariant log symplectic forms on
(\tilde{X},\pi^{-1}(D))
.

Proof.

Bijection between (1) and (2) follows from Proposition
3.9
.
Since
\pi
is smooth there is a canonical isomorphism of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant Lie algebroids

T_{\tilde{X}}\bigl(-\log\pi^{-1}(D)\bigr)\simeq\pi^{!}T_{X}(-\log D).

So Corollary
3.11
gives a bijection between (1) and (3).
∎

3.7
Atiyah extensions on snc pairs

Let
(X,D)
be a projective snc pair.
If
{\mathcal{L}}
is a line bundle on
X
then we usually write
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathcal{L}}}
for the Atiyah bundle
{\mathcal{A}}_{T_{X}}({\mathcal{L}})

and
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathcal{L}}}
for the logarithmic Atiyah bundle
{\mathcal{A}}_{T_{X}(-\log D)}({\mathcal{L}})
.
In the following we write
c_{1}^{\log}({\mathcal{L}})
for
c_{1}^{T_{X}(-\log D)}({\mathcal{L}})
, i.e., for
the image of
c_{1}({\mathcal{L}})
under the canonical map
H^{1}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1})\to H^{1}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D))
.
Similarly, if
\underline{{\mathcal{L}}}
is a collection of line bundles then we write

{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{{\mathcal{L}}}}
for the multi-Atiyah bundle of
\underline{{\mathcal{L}}}
and

{\mathcal{B}}_{\underline{{\mathcal{L}}}}
for the logarithmic multi-Atiyah bundle.

Let
(Y,B)
be another projective snc pair and let
\varphi:X\to Y
be a morphism.
The canonical surjection
\Omega_{X}^{1}\to\Omega^{1}_{X/Y}
allows us to consider
the relative Atiyah extension

0\to\Omega_{X/Y}^{1}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathcal{L}}/Y}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to 0

defined by the pushout of the standard Atiyah extension. The class of this extension in
H^{1}(X,\Omega_{X/Y}^{1})={\mathop{{\rm Ext\,}}}^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X},\Omega_{X/Y}^{1})
is the image of
c_{1}({\mathcal{L}})
under the induced map
H^{1}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1})\to H^{1}(X,\Omega_{X/Y}^{1})
.

Let us write
D=\sum_{i\in I}D_{i}
as a sum of smooth irreducible
D_{i}
. Then for any
J\subset I

we write
D_{J}
for
\bigcap_{j\in J}D_{j}
.
Let us assume that
\varphi
is smooth and the restriction of
\varphi
to each
D_{J}
is also smooth.
Then we can decompose
D
into the horizontal part
D^{h}
corresponding to components that dominate
Y
and the vertical part
D^{v}
that consists of the remaining components. Let us assume that
D^{v}=\varphi^{-1}(B)
.

Then
\Omega^{1}_{X/Y}(\log D^{h})=\Omega^{1}_{X}(\log D^{h})/\varphi^{*}\Omega^{1}_{Y}
is locally free and it fits into the short exact sequence

0\to\Omega^{1}_{X/Y}\to\Omega^{1}_{X/Y}(\log D^{h})\to{\bigoplus}{\mathcal{O}}_{D^{h}_{i}}\to 0

in which
D^{h}_{i}
are the irreducible components of
D^{h}
. Let us also note that
\Omega^{1}_{X/Y}(\log D^{h})

is canonically isomorphic to
\Omega^{1}_{X}(\log D)/\varphi^{*}\Omega^{1}_{Y}(\log B)
.
So as above we can define the relative logarithmic Atiyah extension of
(X,D)/(Y,B)
as the extension

0\to\Omega^{1}_{X/Y}(\log D^{h})\to{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathcal{L}}/Y}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to 0,

whose class in
H^{1}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X/Y}(\log D^{h}))
is the image of
c_{1}^{\log}({\mathcal{L}})
under the canonical map

H^{1}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X}(\log D))\to H^{1}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X/Y}(\log D^{h})).

This extension can be identified with the relative logarithmic Atiyah extension of
{\mathcal{L}}
for
(X,D^{h})/Y
.
By construction we have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega_{X/Y}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathcal{L}}/Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega^{1}_{X/Y}(\log D^{h})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathcal{L}}/Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\bigoplus}{\mathcal{O}}_{D^{h}_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{{\mathop{\rm coker\,}}\alpha\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}

Note also that our definitions imply that the following diagram is commutative:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\varphi^{*}\Omega^{1}_{Y}(\log B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
